- Bring Nature Home: The Biophilic Design You Didn’t Know You Needed!: Kind: captions Language: en [Music] Welcome to Ruong Tropies. Have you ever noticed how your mood lifts when you're surrounded by trees? or how a gentle breeze through an open window can bring a surprising sense of peace. There's something deeply restorative about being close to nature. But in our increasingly urban lives, those connections are growing harder to find. This is where bofilic design steps in. In today's video, we explore the world of biofilic interior design. A growing movement in architecture and home styling that integrates elements of the natural world into our living spaces. This design philosophy isn't just about aesthetics. It's about improving our overall well-being, reducing stress, and creating environments that support both emotional and physical health. Biofilic design is rooted in the idea of biofilia, a term made popular by American biologist Edward O. Wilson in the 1980s. Biofilia describes the innate human instinct to connect with nature and other forms of life. While the concept is ancient, think of traditional homes that used courtyards, natural materials, and open ventilation. It has found renewed relevance in our modern world. In the past few decades, as urbanization has intensified and digital distractions dominate our environments, designers and architects have begun to look for ways to bring nature back into our daily lives. This movement isn't just about adding potted plants to your living room. It's a complete design approach that emphasizes natural light, organic forms, living greenery, earthy materials, and the visual, sensory, and even auditory experiences of the natural world. What began as a design strategy for office buildings and wellness centered public spaces has now found its way into our homes. Whether you're living in a high-rise apartment or a countryside villa, biofilic design is highly adaptable. The goal is simple, to reduce the disconnect between humans and nature by creating spaces that nourish and restore. Imagine sitting in your living room surrounded by large leafy plants, natural wood textures, gentle sunlight pouring through expansive windows, and a direct view of your backyard garden. This isn't a luxury. It's a lifestyle shift and it's available to anyone willing to rethink their space. In this video, we'll take you on a room byroom tour of how bofilic design can transform your home. Starting from the living room, the heart of any house. We'll explore how plants like fiddle leaf figs or monsteras can bring a sense of vitality to the space. Pair those with rot tan furniture, linen textiles, and soft rugs made from jute or cycle, and you instantly invite nature into your surroundings. Maximizing natural sunlight, using large mirrors to reflect outdoor views and incorporating decor with earthy tones and organic patterns further enhances the bofilic feel. Moving into the bedroom, the focus shifts to tranquility. Here, soft sage green walls, cotton or linen bed sheets and air purifying plants like peace liies or snake plants create an environment that encourages deep rest and mental relaxation. Even subtle elements like a ceramic lamp base or the sound of wind chimes outside your window contribute to the calm. The kitchen and dining areas are no less important. These spaces benefit from fresh herb gardens on the windows sill, wooden countertops and centerpieces that change with the seasons. A vase of fresh tulips in spring, dried grasses in autumn, the smell of basil or rosemary, the texture of stone or clay under your hands, the sound of birds chirping just beyond the open door. It all creates an experience that is both functional and deeply fulfilling. Even your bathroom can become a peaceful retreat by using humidityloving plants like ferns or spider plants, incorporating wood or stone fixtures, and using soft earthy scents through essential oil diffusers. You can turn an otherwise sterile room into a rejuvenating spa-like sanctuary. But biofilic design doesn't end indoors. In fact, some of its most powerful expressions happen in the transition between indoors and outdoors. Your front porch or entryway, for instance, sets the tone. Imagine a wooden bench surrounded by potted flowers, a vine covered trellis above, and natural stone steps leading to your door. That's bofilic design in action. Similarly, your backyard or garden can become an immersive oasis with native plants, gravel paths, water features, and soft outdoor lighting. Even small spaces like balconies, window ledges, or rooftop terraces can be turned into vibrant green corners. For those with more room, a rooftop garden with lounge chairs and flowering vines overhead offers a perfect spot to unwind under the sky. There are also lesserknown spaces in your home that benefit tremendously from bofilic elements. Corridors, hallways, and staircases can be enlivened with plant niches, skylights, and wood textures. A reading nook by the window becomes a dreamy hideaway when it's surrounded by climbing plants and flooded with natural light. A meditation space with soft mats, bonsai trees, and calming sense offers a daily escape into serenity. Even utility spaces like laundry rooms can feel welcoming with sunlight and lavender pots on the shelf. And don't worry if your home isn't large. Biofilic design is not about size. It's about intention. It's about using whatever space you have to reconnect with the natural rhythms of life. Whether it's through materials, colors, smells, sounds, or views. This approach gently transforms everyday environments into something more soulful. It reminds us that our homes aren't just shelters, but extensions of our lives and our relationship with the earth. In this episode, we'll also share some practical tips to start your own biofilic transformation. You'll learn how to select the right plants for each room, how to choose organic materials and textures, how to use mirrors and windows strategically, and even how to create visual connections to the outdoors. You'll find out why indirect natural light is just as powerful as a view of greenery and how layering patterns inspired by leaves, bark, or water can subtly make your space feel more alive. Best of all, this transformation doesn't require a complete renovation. Often, it's the small intentional changes. A new curtain that lets in morning light. A shelf repurposed to hold a row of succulents. A color palette shift toward nature inspired tones that have the biggest impact.

- How the Best Brands Use Sensory Marketing to Boost Sales (with Real Brand Examples): Kind: captions Language: en What makes you choose one brand over another? Is it the price, the quality, or maybe simply a feeling? Today, we're going to be talking about what is sensory marketing and how you can use it in your marketing strategy. This is where brands tap into your senses to create unforgettable experiences that drive engagement and boost sales. Let's get into it. What is sensory marketing? Sensory marketing uses sight, sound, smell, taste, and touch to influence consumer behavior. It goes beyond traditional advertising by creating emotional and psychological connections that make a brand more memorable. Studies show that engaging multiple senses can increase brand recall and increase sales. Think about it. Have you ever walked past a bakery and suddenly craved something sweet? Or heard a song that instantly reminded you of a brand? These aren't accidents. They're carefully designed experiences meant to tap into emotions and shape perceptions. Why sensory marketing works. One of the biggest advantages of sensory marketing is its ability to create deep lasting connection with consumers. Unlike traditional marketing, which often relies on logic and persuasion, sensory marketing taps into emotions. People may forget what an ad says, but they rarely forgot how a brand made them feel. Research from Walnut Unlimited found that sensory marketing can increase instore sales by up to 10%. It also revealed that when sensory elements were at play, consumers bought more products at higher prices. All that to say that when people enjoy an immersive experience, they're more willing to invest in it. How brands use sensory marketing. Let's go through examples for each of the five senses. One, sound. From catchy jingles that get stuck in your head to carefully curated background music in stores, brands often use sound to reinforce their identity. Think of McDonald's. Even without the logo, that familiar melody is instantly recognizable. A less specific example of the role of sound in branding is the fact that high-end boutiques play soft, elegant music to create a luxurious atmosphere, while fitness brands use upbeat, energetic tracks to keep customers motivated. Two, site. A brand's colors, logos, and overall aesthetic contribute strongly to its identity. Pronounced visual elements make a brand stand out and create a recognizable look and feel. Luxury brands like Chanel and Louis Vuitton use clean, minimalistic designs to convey exclusivity, while brands like Glossier rely on soft colors and dewy textures to create a sense of approachability and elegance. Three, smell. Smell is directly linked to memory and emotion, making it an incredibly effective marketing tool. Have you ever walked into an Abberrombian Fitch store and instantly recognized a signature scent? That's allactory marketing in action. Some businesses take it even further. Sports retailer Inner Sport, for example, introduced the scent of freshly cut grass in their football section, which led to a big increase in related product sales. The right scent can evoke nostalgia, create comfort, or even encourage spending. Four, touch. Touch is often overlooked, but texture and material quality can influence purchasing decisions. Packaging plays a crucial role here. Brands like Apple use sleek, minimalistic packaging with high quality materials to make unboxing an experience in and of itself. Interactive ads also fall under this category. Some companies create ads where consumers can touch and feel textures or even release scents by scratching the material, making them more memorable and engaging. Five, taste. Taste is a direct and easy way to create positive associations. Restaurants and food brands offer samples to introduce new products and encourage purchases. Some brands take this a step further. One Basque restaurant created edible business cards, turning a simple promotional item into an experience. Sensory marketing in action. Brand examples. Several brands have mastered the art of sensory marketing. Road. Haley Bieber's beauty brand often features its products alongside deserts and social media campaigns. The creamy textures and indulgent visuals tap into both sight and taste, creating a strong emotional pool. Kylie Cosmetics adds a multiensory element to its branding with scented lip products and luxurious soft touch packaging. The brand's collaboration with Seas Candies for a toasted marshmallow lip balm is a great example of this as the PR boxes and experience took sensory marketing to the next level. Glossier is another great example. The brand's minimalist packaging, soft textures, and real life events like exclusive dinners based on their iconic black cherry scent or pop-up shops encourage customers to interact with products firsthand, reinforcing brand loyalty. Lush takes sensory marketing to the next level. Their stores are a full sensory experience. From strong fragrances to colorful displays and interactive product demos, this brand can keep customers engaged both in store and online. The rise of experiential marketing. As we've said, sensory marketing isn't just about products. It's about creating experiences. Experiential marketing, which focus on immersing customers in a brand's world, has become increasingly popular. Branded pop-up stores filled with flowers, are one example of this. Many brands have recently begun incorporating flowers into their temporary displays, promoting both beauty and strategic thinking. Lauro Piana transformed kiosk in Milan into floral art installations, combining visual and allactory marketing to create an elegant interactive brand experience. Tiffany and Co. partnered with Flower Box to turn its temporary flagship into a floral paradise, aligning the brand with luxury and romance. Jagumus even launched a flower shop in Paris where bouques are wrapped in recycled fabric from previous fashion collections, blending sustainability, visual appeal, and a tactile experience. A few years later, the brand opened a similar flower pop-up store in Seoul. In a world where brands fight for attention, sensory marketing stands out. Instead of ads, people scroll past. It creates real connections through touch, scent, sound, and immersive experiences that leave a lasting impression. If you're looking for more ways to boost your brand success, check out our free ebook on 50 digital marketing strategies to launch your product by clicking the link in the description. It's packed with practical tips to help you nail your next launch. Also, don't forget to like and subscribe to Marketing Explained so you never miss a video.

- What is cocooning ? : Navigating  life after adoption: Kind: captions Language: en If you're thinking about adopting or maybe you've already brought home your sweet child, then this video is for you because today we are talking about what happens after you bring home this child of yours and the whole process of bonding, cocooning, and what that is, what that looks like, and how to do it. You are already figuring all this out before you have brought home that sweet child, then you're already to a better start than I was. Hi, my name is Sierra McDowell and thank you so much for being here. It really means a lot to me. So, today we're going to be talking about what cocooning is, what it looks like, and what's going to fit for your family. I'm also going to be sharing a little bit of the mistakes that we made in the whole process after we brought our son home because, well, we just didn't know what the heck we were doing to be honest. I will also share at the end of this the test that they did on my son. Test. They did on my son to see kind of how he was doing with this. So, the word cocooning. That is to surround in a protective way or a comforting way. It's a period of time where the family intentionally pulls back from their surroundings and their busy life and all the things that they might have going on with your full attention and being able to focus on the bonding without many other distractions so that that attachment with you and with them can take place. With this you are purposely making your world smaller in order for there to be room for bonding and safety to to flourish. And you will really just focus in on meeting that child's needs. So, if you have a younger one, really trying to be like you doing a lot of hands-on feeding, you being the only ones that's changing diapers, not really having friends and family over, not encouraging them to go and spend time in bonding with anyone else. Um, you being the one changing them when they fall, then you're the one that comforts them so that they know that you are the parent. So, why is cocooning important? Well, if you think about it, in instances where a child isn't adopted at birth, you lose some of that initial bonding time. So, they don't really know how to learn to trust those relationships, especially because relationships are scary because of most of them have been lost from the very beginning. With all that brokenness, over time it really starts to wear on the psyche of a child and their heart and being able to understand what it is to have relationships that you can trust and feel safe within because there's been so much loss from them in the past that stepping into another relationship can be a very frightening thing. So, what does cocooning actually look like? Well, it looks different for every family and it varies, especially depending on, you know, what your child's come from, you know, how you guys are doing as a family. So, some people do it for a couple of weeks. Some people do it for a few months and some people do it for a year. So, it looks different for, you know, if you're bringing home a teenager or if you're bringing home a little one, like a toddler. So, our toddler, we were trying to do all of those things. With the teenager, you can't really do that as much where you're like, "Here, let me feed you." It's going to come off kind of creepy and this kid's going to be like, "What the heck did I just get myself into?" So, with a teenager, it might be, you know, not having as many people over, not going out and doing as many things, not, you know, signing them up for all this different stuff and really just taking some time to have them home, not sending them to school right away, and um making it a focal point of, you know, everything else does not matter as much as this time together right now. So, even if you did the cocooning for a few weeks or a few months and then later you start looking at some signs and seeing a couple things where you think, "Okay, it might be time to do this again." That is common. We actually had to do that a couple of times where we were like, "No, we're pulling it back in." And then we just stayed there for a while. And there's just a couple signs to just be looking out for in your child. So, for some people this seems like it is like, of course, why would we not do this? This is perfect. And then for others it's like, "Oh my gosh, this is going to be really hard." Um, and but it is extremely beneficial and it will just help your relationship so much in the beginning. So, with this, I'm going to tell you how we screwed up so bad in this area because, I don't know, we just didn't know what the heck we were doing. And that's why I wanted to make this for you so you don't make the same mistakes that we made. So, when we got home, we are like extreme extroverts. We have people over all the time. Our house is a revolving door. People drop in all the time and family lives close by. So, we got home and after spending 5 weeks in another country, we were like, "Yes, everyone, we want to see you." And we were excited when he was like, you know, going near other people and kind of interacting with them and we were letting other people, you know, hand him food and things like that. And this was just all a terrible, terrible, terrible idea. So, we did that for probably like 2 weeks and then we went to a an appointment with our international adoption doctors and she's great, but like we kind of left feeling like we were screwing up our relationship with our son and we were wondering if we could ever repair the damage that we had already done by not cocooning like we should have. And so, at that time we decided like we really need to bring this back in. We are the only ones changing his diapers. We are the only ones, you know, feeding him. No one else could give him the food. I would carry him constantly and I did that before, um but I would just, you know, make it a point of just strapping him on me and just kind of taking him wherever I go. And we were just trying to meet all of those needs and we told everyone that they needed to step back. Yes, we still had people come over because we know that like we, for our sanity, need to have people. So, we just created some boundaries. Like, if he would fall, like we would be like, "No, sorry. We didn't leave him with anyone for a long time except for my mom. So, then we got to speak with an attachment specialist therapist, which if you can find one, it was so extremely helpful and she was like, "No, guys, you can dial it back a little bit and it is okay to, you know, relax a little bit." We found it was important to ensure that our cocoon partially mirrored the safe environment our little person had come from, using the same washing powder for familiar smells, valuing items they brought with them, no matter how few, keeping initial habits no matter how much we wanted to change them in the longer term.   3. @NovasYouTubeName: 3:02 though everyone keep in mind, older kids and teens being adopted will sometime need to “fill in the gaps” they missed and want to be fed and tickled and snuggled. Their brains can blip into “regressions” that are actually just the healing process because their brain is catching up on things lost in their younger years. - How to Incorporate Natural Elements Into Your Home | HGTV: Kind: captions Language: en ♪♪

- Why are we so attached to our things? - Christian Jarrett: Kind: captions Language: en After witnessing the violent rage shown by babies whenever deprived of an item they considered their own, Jean Piaget, a founding father of child psychology, observed something profound about human nature. Our sense of ownership emerges incredibly early. Why are we so clingy? There's a well-established phenomenon in psychology known as the endowment effect where we value items much more highly just as soon as we own them. In one famous demonstration, students were given a choice between a coffee mug or a Swiss chocolate bar as a reward for helping out with research. Half chose the mug, and half chose the chocolate. That is, they seemed to value the two rewards similarly. Other students were given a mug first and then a surprise chance to swap it for a chocolate bar, but only 11% wanted to. Yet another group started out with chocolate, and most preferred to keep it rather than swap. In other words, the students nearly always put greater value on whichever reward they started out with. Part of this has to do with how quickly we form connections between our sense of self and the things we consider ours. That can even be seen at the neural level. In one experiment, neuroscientists scanned participants' brains while they allocated various objects either to a basket labeled "mine," or another labeled, "Alex's." When participants subsequently looked at their new things, their brains showed more activity in a region that usually flickers into life whenever we think about ourselves. Another reason we're so fond of our possessions is that from a young age we believe they have a unique essence. Psychologists showed us this by using an illusion to convince three to six-year-olds they built a copying machine, a device that could create perfect replicas of any item. When offered a choice between their favorite toy or an apparently exact copy, the majority of the children favored the original. In fact, they were often horrified at the prospect of taking home a copy. This magical thinking about objects isn't something we grow out of. Rather it persists into adulthood while becoming ever more elaborate. For example, consider the huge value placed on items that have been owned by celebrities. It's as if the buyers believed the objects they'd purchased were somehow imbued with the essence of their former celebrity owners. For similar reasons, many of us are reluctant to part with family heirlooms which help us feel connected to lost loved ones. These beliefs can even alter our perception of the physical world and change our athletic abilities. Participants in a recent study were told they were using a golf putter once owned by the champion Ben Curtis. During the experiment, they perceived the hole as being about a centimeter larger than controlled participants using a standard putter and they sank slightly more putts. Although feelings of ownership emerge early in life, culture also plays a part. For example, it was recently discovered that Hadza people of northern Tanzania who are isolated from modern culture don't exhibit the endowment effect. That's possibly because they live in an egalitarian society where almost everything is shared. At the other extreme, sometimes our attachment to our things can go too far. Part of the cause of hoarding disorder is an exaggerated sense of responsibility and protectiveness toward one's belongings. That's why people with this condition find it so difficult to throw anything away. What remains to be seen today is how the nature of our relationship with our possessions will change with the rise of digital technologies. Many have forecast the demise of physical books and music, but for now, at least, this seems premature. Perhaps there will always be something uniquely satisfying about holding an object in our hands and calling it our own.

- Can You Overcome Emotional Attachment To Belongings? - Minimalist Home Life: Kind: captions Language: en Can you overcome emotional attachment to belongings? Imagine holding onto an object for years, feeling a deep sense of comfort or identity tied to it. Many people experience this kind of emotional connection to their belongings, whether it is a childhood toy, a piece of jewelry, or even a collection of books. But is it possible to change these feelings and let go of possessions that no longer serve a purpose? Research indicates that emotional attachment to belongings is rooted in psychological and neurological factors. When individuals associate objects with memories, milestones, or feelings of safety, their brain creates a bond that can be difficult to break. The hippocampus, a part of the brain involved in memory formation, plays a significant role in linking objects to personal history. Additionally, the amigdala, which processes emotions, reinforces these attachments through feelings of comfort or nostalgia. Overcoming such attachments involves understanding the underlying reasons for holding onto possessions. Often these objects symbolize relationships, achievements or periods of life that a person wishes to preserve. Recognizing that the emotional value is rooted in memories rather than the physical object itself can be a crucial step. Cognitive behavioral strategies can help individuals reframe their thoughts about possessions, emphasizing the importance of memories over material items. Practical approaches include gradually decluttering by setting specific goals such as removing one item per week. Creating a designated space for sentimental objects can help manage emotional responses. Engaging in mindfulness practices can also increase awareness of feelings that arise during the process, allowing individuals to address them consciously. It is important to note that overcoming emotional attachment is a gradual process that varies from person to person. Support from friends, family or mental health professionals can provide encouragement and guidance. Ultimately, while the emotional bonds to belongings are strong, they are not unbreakable. With patience, self-awareness, and intentional effort, it is entirely possible to reduce these attachments and foster a sense of freedom from material possessions.

- You’ve Never Seen an Eco Home Like This Cocoon House (House Tour): Kind: captions Language: en The house is based on a curve shape, which not&nbsp; only abstracts the reflections coming in from the skylights but it makes water&nbsp;reflections on the inside of the curve sometimes and people have said&nbsp; they feel like they're inside an ocean wave. My name is Nina Edwards Anker and I'm the&nbsp; founder of Nea studio and this is the Cocoon House. The house is located in Long Island&nbsp; close to the beach on a compound property shared with my brother and the&nbsp; house is very much shaped by the site. So this house is based on my PhD thesis which I wrote in Scandinavia which is very much about the house being small in&nbsp; a vast, natural setting. Experiencing biophilic design with all the five senses which then&nbsp; is very connected to our minds and our well-being. One of the ideas that also drove&nbsp;this house was the idea of effect and affect so in other words the experience of the&nbsp; house was the most important and it was about the sunlight coming through at different&nbsp; times of day and then the colors of the skylights range from bright yellow which is&nbsp; the zenith of the sun according to Goethe's color theory and then through orangey tones down to&nbsp; vermilion red which is the color of the sunset. In terms of the house plan as you move up&nbsp; the driveway, the approach to the house is through the backside on the North and come&nbsp; into an open living space where there's a hearth and a dining table behind which&nbsp; there is an open kitchen and then down the corridor to access the bedroom wing. After the laundry we have the two children's rooms and each child gets a mezzanine and then&nbsp; we have the main bedroom which is my favourite place. It has a view towards the ocean and over&nbsp; the open lawn where you can see the sea breeze coming through the trees. There's a lot of&nbsp; wildlife, many deer and foxes and turkeys and birds of different sorts so it's a real pleasure&nbsp; to be able to look out onto the wetland and its teeming wildlife. A sliding door from the main&nbsp; bedroom leads out to a patio where there is a reflecting pond or cistern which reflects the&nbsp;sunsets in the winter and provides cooling in the summertime. So this house takes a Scandinavian&nbsp; approach to architecture where the house is entirely shaped actually by the&nbsp; site and so it takes cues from local architectural typologies which is in&nbsp; Long Island, the houses are covered with cedar shingles, so we take the cedar&nbsp; shingles to fit into the neighbourhood typology and the inside structure of the house is&nbsp;a bent wood which recalls also the cedar saplings that were bent to form the wigwams. All of&nbsp;the materials in the house are LEED certified. The bathrooms are made of Imperial White Danby Vermont&nbsp; marble and the floors are local oak, the patios are local blue stone as is the surround for the&nbsp;hearth in the kitchen counter. As the mother of a cancer survivor I'm very aware&nbsp; of the natural and non-toxic nature of my interiors and all of the materials for the beds&nbsp; and the upholstered furniture are within 500 miles distance according to LEED protocol. The cistern which is at the foot of the windows also collects 50% of the rainwater according&nbsp; to LEED protocol which is recirculated into a non-chlorine filtering system. So&nbsp;the house is based on very basic, passive house principles and I believe that this is the&nbsp; way that we can make change happen faster by incorporating environmental technologies into our&nbsp; everyday lives that actually help us to enjoy our spaces. When I look around this project I am&nbsp;most proud of the people who enjoy the space. This house was designed for people it was designed for my children and I&nbsp; like to bring in the community and to hopefully spread the knowledge about&nbsp; environmental design and building with nature. - What’s Inside A Caterpillar 'Cocoon?': Kind: captions Language: en What would you do for the power to fly? How about shedding your skin and dissolving your own muscles? Now, believe it or not, that gruesome process is how caterpillars earn their wings. Here's what you might not know about what's inside a caterpillar's "cocoon." Contrary to popular belief, this is not a cocoon. Only certain moths build cocoons, which are like a silky sleeping bag that covers the insect. This, on the other hand, is what's called a chrysalis. It's not a sack or a pouch; it's actually the caterpillar's own body. When it's time for the transformation to begin, the caterpillar's body ramps up production of a hormone called ecdysone, and that causes it to cast off its outer coating, sort of like how a snake sheds its skin. And underneath is a hard shell similar to the exoskeleton of a beetle. After that, life for the little caterpillar gets oozy. First, it releases enzymes called caspases. These rip apart and dissolve cells in its muscles, digestive system, and other organs. But the enzymes don't quite liquefy all of the caterpillar. They leave key structures intact, like breathing tubes. At the same time, specialized cells called imaginal discs start waking up. Before the chrysalis stage, these discs were kept dormant by a series of hormones in the caterpillar's body. But once the transformation begins, those hormone levels take a nosedive, giving these discs the opportunity to do what they do best: build a butterfly. You see, each disc contains the genetic recipe to form a different adult body part, starting from the inside out. After one week, the digestive system of the butterfly is well on its way. And by day 16, the adult's legs, wings, eyes, and mouth are all present and in working order. Now, two weeks is a remarkably short time for all of this to happen, since each imaginal disc starts out with only about 50 cells and must multiply those into thousands just to form a single wing. And if you checked out the chrysalis around day 16, you might even be able to see those brilliantly colored wings. Because for some species, their chrysalis turns transparent in their final days of metamorphosis. Now, fully formed, it's time to hit the road. The chrysalis splits open down the center, and the butterfly escapes. Meanwhile, a reddish liquid spills out. That's all the waste the butterfly, née caterpillar, produced during its stay. Once its wings expand and harden, it's ready to mate, pollinate, and slurp nectar to its heart's desire. But one of the most interesting parts of all? Research suggests that butterflies and moths can remember their caterpillar days. In one study, researchers trained moth caterpillars to associate an odor with an electric shock, so whenever the larvae smelled it, they'd move away. But even after they transformed into adult moths, they still avoided the scary smell. It makes you wonder what else they could recall from their younger days.

Is your home a sanctuary where you find your true self? Or is it a high-end prison designed by your own nervous system to keep you from the terror of being seen? Today, we unmask the deep psychology of the home body. From the elite villas of ancient Rome to the disturbing hallucination experiments of the 1950s, why do some people feel a spark of energy in a crowd while others feel their battery draining with every hello? The answer lies in optimal arousal theory. Science suggests that the nervous systems of home oriented personalities are hardwired differently. If you are a home body, your brain likely requires less external stimulation to reach a state of contentment. For you, a buzzing neon sign or a sharp overhead lamp isn't just a nuisance. He described his Laurentine villa not as a house but as a person who returns a kindness, a social contract of peace. For a Roman, the dois was a universal haven. I'm keeping tonight quiet or I can do an hour. By honoring your neurological need for stillness, you develop a form of stoic detachment from social pressure. - How Mountains Of Worm Cocoons Are Turned Into Expensive Silk In Vietnam | Big Business: Kind: captions Language: en-US Narrator: Each of these cocoons holds almost 1,000 feet of silk. And these wiggly little guys are responsible for spinning them. Here in Nam Cao, Vietnam, craftspeople weave silk that's so valuable, it can sell for $150 a dress. That's because this is really delicate work. Each cocoon has to be hand-pulled, and the worms themselves are really hard to raise. For one, they're always hungry. And they need the perfect environment, or else they'll die. Today, Vietnam is the second-biggest producer of raw silk in the world. Nearly everyone in this village works in the industry, and they're using many of the same techniques passed down for generations. But about a decade ago, the industry in this town almost dried up. Narrator: Fake silk from China had flooded the market, and warmer temperatures were threatening the worms' lives. But this area revived itself thanks to Hạnh She got farmers to weave more profitable goods and helped them reinvest in new technologies that boosted production. Narrator: We head to Vietnam to see how one village saved its big business of silk. Silk villages dot Vietnam. And they produce silk in a tedious 30-step process. It all starts in fields like this one. There aren't any worms out here. This is just their food, and they need this much of it. Narrator: These leaves contain vitamins, amino acids, and enough moisture that the worms never need to drink water. Farmers bring loads of harvested leaves to the silkworm houses. Narrator: Trần Văn Thịnh is a 35-year veteran farmer. He buys tens of thousands of worms from breeders each year, and he raises them on big bamboo mats on the first floor of his home. When it's feeding time, he lays a net on the worms. Then, he heaps 3 pounds of food on top. The healthy worms crawl through the holes and get to munching. Narrator: And they eat over 600 pounds of leaves a day. Narrator: Meaning Thịnh can't rest, because the worms eat every four hours. Narrator: They also need a sterile environment and a perfect temperature of around 22 degrees Celsius. Narrator: After nearly a month of eating and sleeping, their bodies will plump up. Narrator: Thịnh delivers the worms to another family in Hong Phong. They hang them on racks stuffed with straw. The worms spit the silk out of this organ, called a spinneret. During this vulnerable time, workers fight off bees that attack the silkworms. Narrator: The worms need sunny weather to produce colorful cocoons, because if it's rainy and gray outside, the cocoons could lose their color and look damaged. Within two days, the cocoons are ready. Workers remove each one by hand. Once they've got a big enough pile, they boil the cocoons in water. That way, they can kill the pupae insides and either eat them or use them in traditional medicine. Narrator: Then they soak the cocoons for another five to seven hours before pulling the threads. Narrator: Families here pass down the delicate technique of hand-pulling thread. Narrator: Phạm Thị Buông has worked in silk for nearly 60 years. She'll unravel about 70 grams of thread a day. Narrator: This machine reels the silk. Workers then hang the threads to dry. Narrator: Next, the threads head to the cooperative to be woven. Narrator: And finally, the silk gets dunked a hundred times in all-natural dyes. They use gac fruit for red, almond leaves or turmeric for yellow, tallow leaves for black, and Indigofera for, well, indigo. It's the same technique they've used for generations. Silk weaving dates back 4,000 years in Vietnam, all the way to the Bronze Age. For millennia, it was the fabric of choice for nobility. And in its heyday in the 16th to 18th century, traders came from all over the world to buy Vietnam's silk. Beginning in the 2000s, Vietnam's temperatures heated up, and storms came more frequently. Narrator: There was also an influx of counterfeit silk from China. Narrator: Real silk will burn slowly and smell like burnt hair, while the fake stuff will burn quickly and smell like plastic. Prices for Vietnam's silk fluctuated, dropping as low as $3 per kilo. With less profit, young people took jobs in textile factories in the cities to earn more. Narrator: Hạnh heard the story of the diminishing craft village when she visited in the early 2010s, and she wanted to help. She quit her job in interior design and decided to band families together. Narrator: She got farmers to purchase air-conditioning units so they could raise worms in any weather. Narrator: They went from being able to produce 440 to 600 pounds of cocoons per year to over 2,200 pounds. Hạnh introduced benefits for the farmers, like holidays off and helping stabilize the price for silk. Narrator: Today, over 200 families in Nam Cao are part of her cooperative, pumping out 220 pounds of silk a month. And each family can make up to $12,000 a year, a high wage for this area. They weave a silk that is great at moisture and temperature control, making it breathable in the summer and insulating in the winter. Because of these properties, silk from Hạnh's collective can be used in everything from sofa covers, curtains, and suits to pillowcases and sheets. Narrator: Hạnh hopes the success of the Nam Cao cooperative will attract young talent back into the industry so farmers can reinvest in the town. Narrator: Until then, they'll keep spinning away.

of an open plan layout simply do outweigh the cons now unless you have a carpeted home&nbsp; it's likely that you've considered putting&nbsp;&nbsp; a rug underfoot in your living area and what this&nbsp; essentially is is a large piece of loose carpet&nbsp;&nbsp; usually in in a rectangular shape left unfixed to&nbsp; the floor however there is almost unlimited Choice&nbsp;&nbsp; when it comes to a rug's material size shape and&nbsp; color firstly choosing to have a rug in your home&nbsp;&nbsp; does a number of things it creates a little bit&nbsp; of warmth and softness underf foot which is a lot&nbsp;&nbsp; more comfortable to walk on and it also helps to&nbsp; absorb a lot of the acoustic reverberations that&nbsp;&nbsp; tends to bounce off hard surfaces which can make&nbsp; rooms feel a little bit Hollow when doing things&nbsp;&nbsp; like watching TV or holding a conversation but&nbsp; where some materials are even prone to deforming&nbsp;&nbsp; when they come into contact with water which does&nbsp; make some rug color and material combinations&nbsp;&nbsp; particularly flawed in logic So to avoid mistakes&nbsp; when it comes to rugs make sure that you buy&nbsp;&nbsp; the right size as proportions are incredibly&nbsp; important and then on top of this if you can go&nbsp;&nbsp; for a textured or a patterned rug these tend to be&nbsp; a lot more forgiving when it comes to staining as well now it goes without saying that as soon as&nbsp; night falls we can no longer rely on our Windows&nbsp;&nbsp; to provide us with light so because of this most&nbsp; efficient and unobtrusive way to provide light to&nbsp;&nbsp; a room and when compared to other types of light&nbsp; fixtures these save on space and cost and really&nbsp;&nbsp; they just fulfill the basic need of being able&nbsp; to see when it's dark but the thing is when it&nbsp;&nbsp; comes to living areas is a ceiling light fixture&nbsp; can feel harsh which stops a space feeling cozy&nbsp;&nbsp; and doesn't really flatter anyone or anything&nbsp; inside of it which can make you want to spend&nbsp;&nbsp; less time in the space but these days when it&nbsp; comes to lighting efficiency is a lot less of&nbsp;&nbsp; a concern as with the Advent of LEDs these have&nbsp;   9. @nathanlonghair: I know that Americans are crazy about candles, so if you do feel a candle is important to your interior:
1) Use pure beeswax. No scent, no cheap paraffin. VOC's/particles that are bad for your respiratory system are more concentrated in paraffin burning, especially for scented candles.
2) Do it when you have (non-asthmatic) guests, or for special occasions, don't always have one on. You'll likely have to limit it anyway since beeswax is expensive.
3) Trim the wick to a quarter inch before lighting; less soot for a while.
4) Crack a window when burning a candle and air out especially well while extinguishing it. Ventilation is key to limiting impact.

But even better: Get some of the good LED candles, with rechargeable batteries. No indoor emissions, can last months on a charge, can be set to turn on at regular schedules. When you get some of the good ones that simulate candle flicker well, you can get almost exactly the same effect without damaging your lungs over time. Especially if you have kids.
Stick to the ones without scent again, and you can get them with a paraffin exterior so it looks very close to a real candle. I find the ones with a little flat white plastic flame that physically moves gives the best simulation - the ones that are a 3D flame shape where the light inside itself flashes typically aren't as great.

If you want scented because your place doesn't smell great, take out your trash, wash your clothes, clean your drains, and air out regularly.
Even regular exposure to a single crappy candle without ventilation can have noticeable health effects down the road.

But I mean... you CAN get great lighting and a great pad without candles at all - just sayin'.

- Biophilic design: Nature in our interiors: Kind: captions Language: en Biophilic design An approach to interior design that focuses&nbsp; on the connection between humans and nature. It helps us feel better, increases our&nbsp; productivity and contributes to sustainability. Welcome to today's video! In this video, we'll&nbsp; look at the history and importance of biophilic&nbsp;&nbsp; design, the basic principles, how to apply it&nbsp; in your home or work environment, and showcase&nbsp;&nbsp; inspiring examples from around the world. Let's get started!&nbsp; Although biophilic design may sound like a&nbsp; new concept, its roots go deep into the past.&nbsp; With the development of modern&nbsp; design and urbanisation,&nbsp;&nbsp; we have become increasingly isolated from nature.&nbsp;&nbsp; Fortunately, in recent decades, we have begun to&nbsp; return to natural elements in our environment.&nbsp; Biophilic design has many positive impacts on our&nbsp; health and well-being. By incorporating natural&nbsp;&nbsp; elements into our interiors, we reduce stress,&nbsp; improve air quality and increase our well-being.&nbsp; There is a lot of research that confirms&nbsp; the positive influence of nature on us.&nbsp; For example, one study found&nbsp; that patients in hospitals&nbsp;&nbsp; with green views recovered faster&nbsp; than those with views of buildings.&nbsp; Now that we've learned about the history&nbsp; and importance of biophilic design,&nbsp;&nbsp; let's take a look at its basic principles&nbsp; and how we can apply them to our interiors.&nbsp; Biophilic design is based&nbsp; on three main principles:&nbsp; Direct contact with nature, indirect contact with&nbsp; nature and spatial and architectural solutions.&nbsp; Direct contact with nature includes the presence&nbsp; of plants, natural light and water in the&nbsp;&nbsp; interiors. This may include windows overlooking&nbsp; green spaces, gardens, terraces or aquariums.&nbsp; Indirect contact with nature includes the use&nbsp; of natural materials, textures and colours in&nbsp;&nbsp; the interior. This may include wooden floors,&nbsp; stone walls or textiles with natural motifs.&nbsp; Spatial and architectural solutions include&nbsp; biomimicry, natural patterns and proportions&nbsp;&nbsp; in construction. These can be roof gardens,&nbsp; facades of greenery or organic furniture shapes.&nbsp; Now we will look at some tips and advice on how to&nbsp;&nbsp; get started with biophilic design&nbsp; in your home or work environment. Incorporating live plants and&nbsp; natural materials into your décor: Start by incorporating live plants into your&nbsp;&nbsp; spaces to improve air quality&nbsp; and add life to the interior.&nbsp;&nbsp; In addition, use natural materials such as wood,&nbsp; stone or wool in furniture and accessories. Create a connection with the&nbsp; outdoors (windows, patios, gardens)&nbsp; Create a connection with the outdoors&nbsp; through windows overlooking green spaces,&nbsp;&nbsp; terraces or balconies that offer access to nature.&nbsp;&nbsp; Also consider creating an indoor garden&nbsp; or conservatory as part of your home.&nbsp; Choose natural colours and textures to&nbsp; create a sense of relaxation and calm&nbsp; Be guided by nature when choosing&nbsp; colours and textures. Choose muted,&nbsp;&nbsp; natural shades such as greens, browns or blues&nbsp; to create a sense of calm and relaxation.&nbsp;&nbsp; Also use fabrics with natural patterns and&nbsp; motifs such as leaves, flowers or water features.&nbsp; Now we'll take a look at some great examples and&nbsp; inspirations that show what biophilic design looks&nbsp;&nbsp; like in practice. You can get inspiration&nbsp; for your own home or work environment.&nbsp; Here we see a living room where biophilic&nbsp; design is used to highlight natural elements.&nbsp;&nbsp; A large window to the garden, natural&nbsp; materials such as wood and stone,&nbsp;&nbsp; and green plants create a calming&nbsp; atmosphere in which we feel great.&nbsp; In this office, we can see how biophilic design&nbsp; aids employee productivity and well-being. Green&nbsp;&nbsp; walls, natural materials and spaces for relaxation&nbsp; increase concentration and satisfaction.&nbsp; Restaurants can also create a unique&nbsp; atmosphere by using biophilic design.&nbsp;&nbsp; This restaurant uses green walls, wooden&nbsp; elements and natural light to create a&nbsp;&nbsp; welcoming environment where guests can&nbsp; enjoy their meal in harmony with nature.&nbsp; You can apply the biophilic design to any room&nbsp; in your home, from the bedroom to the bathroom or&nbsp;&nbsp; the work area. By using natural materials, plants&nbsp; and natural light, you can create an environment&nbsp;&nbsp; that calms you and promotes your well-being. In addition to its positive impact on our&nbsp;&nbsp; health and well-being, biophilic&nbsp; design can also contribute to&nbsp;&nbsp; sustainability and environmental friendliness. Use recycled or sustainably sourced materials:&nbsp; By using recycled or sustainably sourced&nbsp; materials, we can make a significant contribution&nbsp;&nbsp; to the sustainability of our interiors. Let's take&nbsp; a look at some examples of how to achieve this.&nbsp; FSC-certified wood guarantees that the wood&nbsp; comes from responsibly managed forests.&nbsp;&nbsp; By using this wood in the production of&nbsp; furniture, flooring or decorative elements,&nbsp;&nbsp; we support sustainable forest management. Recycled materials can be used to make&nbsp;&nbsp; furniture or decorations. This reduces&nbsp; waste and saves the planet's resources.&nbsp; Another option is sustainably sourced materials&nbsp; such as bamboo, cork wood or linen fabrics.&nbsp;&nbsp; These materials have less environmental&nbsp; impact than traditional building materials,&nbsp;&nbsp; while providing a beautiful and natural look. Irrigation and energy-saving solutions&nbsp;&nbsp; for plants and lighting: We will now look at irrigation and&nbsp;&nbsp; energy saving solutions that can help conserve&nbsp; natural resources and reduce energy costs.&nbsp; When it comes to plants, you can introduce&nbsp; water-saving irrigation systems such as drip&nbsp;&nbsp; irrigation or automatic watering. These systems&nbsp; help keep plants healthy and save water by&nbsp;&nbsp; delivering it directly to the roots of the plants. When it comes to lighting, energy-efficient&nbsp;&nbsp; solutions such as LED bulbs can significantly&nbsp; reduce the energy consumption of the interior.&nbsp;&nbsp; LED bulbs consume less energy and have a&nbsp; longer lifespan than traditional bulbs.&nbsp; Finally, let's not forget the importance of&nbsp; natural lighting. Large windows and glazed doors&nbsp;&nbsp; can provide plenty of daylight and contribute&nbsp; to energy savings on artificial lighting.&nbsp; Support local craftsmen and&nbsp; artists in interior design:&nbsp; It is also important to support local&nbsp; artisans and artists in interior design.&nbsp; Supporting local craftsmen and artists&nbsp; in the selection of interior decoration&nbsp;&nbsp; and furniture has several benefits. Firstly, this reduces the environmental&nbsp;&nbsp; impact associated with transport, as&nbsp; the products are sourced from nearby.&nbsp; And secondly, we support the local economy&nbsp; and contribute to our community by buying&nbsp;&nbsp; from local artists and craftspeople. Biophilic design is gaining popularity&nbsp;&nbsp; and becoming a key aspect of sustainable&nbsp; design, bringing about changes in&nbsp;&nbsp; architecture and interior design. Biophilic design is also gaining&nbsp;&nbsp; popularity in commercial and public spaces,&nbsp; demonstrating its wide range of applications.&nbsp; In the future, we can expect innovations&nbsp; and technologies such as virtual gardens,&nbsp;&nbsp; intelligent plants and advanced&nbsp; materials that mimic natural properties.&nbsp; Think about your own relationship&nbsp; with nature and consider how you can&nbsp;&nbsp; improve your environment, wellbeing and&nbsp; sustainability through biophilic design.&nbsp; Thank you for joining our exploration&nbsp;&nbsp; of the world of biophilic design today." Today we've explored the importance of biophilic&nbsp;&nbsp; design for human health and well-being, practical&nbsp; tips for applying biophilic design indoors,&nbsp;&nbsp; inspiration from real-world examples, and the&nbsp; importance of sustainability in biophilic design.&nbsp; Let us know what your experiences with&nbsp; biophilic design are and what topics you&nbsp;&nbsp; would like us to cover in the future. Don't&nbsp; forget to leave a comment below the video.&nbsp; If you liked this video, don't forget to subscribe&nbsp; to our channel and watch for more videos on&nbsp;&nbsp; similar topics that we will add regularly.&nbsp; We look forward to seeing you next time!

- Living With Nature Effortless Biophilic Design for Modern Homes: Kind: captions Language: en [Music] Biofilic design is more than just a design trend. It's a way of life. At its core, it's about reconnecting with nature in our daily living spaces. In a world that often feels fast-paced and stressful, bringing nature indoors helps create balance, calm, and effortless beauty that truly feels like home. Biofilic design stems from the idea idea that humans are innately connected to nature. By weaving natural materials, greenery, sunlight, and organic patterns into modern homes, we nourish that bond. This connection doesn't just beautify interiors. It enhances our mental well-being, reduces stress, and fosters a sense of belonging within our spaces. Heat. Heat. Modern homes are often sleek, minimal, and efficient, but they can also feel cold and disconnected. Biofilic design softens this by layering warmth, texture, and life. It ensures our homes don't just function, but also nurture us. In today's eco-conscious world, it's the perfect blend of comfort and responsibility. Sunlight is the most powerful ful natural element we can bring indoors. Large windows, skylights, and glass walls flood spaces with daylight, making rooms feel expansive and uplifting. Exposure to natural light supports circadian rhythms, improves mood, and enhances focus, transforming the home into a vibrant and healthy living environment. Hey. Ventilation is a cornerstone of bofilic design. Operable windows, sliding doors, and open floor plans allow for natural air flow. Integrating plants that purify the air amplifies this effect. Fresh air circulation not only improves health but also keeps the home connected to the rhythms of the natural environment outside. House plants are not just decor. They are living, breathing elements that change the entire energy of a room. From statement indoor trees to cascading vines, greenery softens sharp edges of modern design. They reduce toxins, improve air quality, and add layers of texture, creating an effortless harmony between luxury and life. Water features bring fluidity and serenity indoors. A small fountain, indoor pond, or even an aquarium introduces soothing sounds and reflective movement that instantly relaxes the mind. Just as forests calm us with flowing streams, water elements in a home offer peace, luxury, and a deeper connection to nature. [Applause] Wood, stone, clay, and bamboo are timeless materials that enrich spaces with authenticity. Unlike synthetic finishes, organic textures age gracefully and carry unique stories within their grains and patterns. Integrating these materials in floors, furniture, or accents allows nature's character to shine, giving homes depth and soul. Heat. Nature is full of repeating patterns like waves, leaves, and honeycombs. By echoing these shapes in wallpapers, textiles, and architectural details, we create visual harmony. These patterns subconsciously remind us of the outdoors, creating a sense of calm. In biophilic design, even the smallest natural motif strengthens the overall connection. Colors have a powerful emotional impact. Bofilic homes often draw from earthy tones, greens, warm browns, sandy beiges, and sky inspired blues. These hues soothe the senses and make spaces feel grounded. When paired with natural textures, the palette evokes an effortless luxury rooted in simplicity and balance. Hey, hey, hey. Bofilic design thrives when boundaries between inside and outside fade away. Sliding glass doors, open patios, and balconies extend the living area into nature. This fluidity encourages outdoor living while keeping nature close. By merging these spaces, modern homes feel more expansive, lively, and deeply connected to their surroundings. architecture plays a vital role in biophilic living. Curved lines inspired by landscapes, atriums filled with greenery, and open courtyards embody the design philosophy. Architects are increasingly designing homes that invite daylight, wind, and vegetation inside so that natural elements aren't additions, but integral parts of the home's DNA. Minimalist homes can sometimes feel stark, but biophilia softens that austerity. A simple layout with intentional natural accents like one statement tree, a stone feature wall, or natural fabrics creates balance. Instead of clutter, the focus shifts to meaningful organic beauty. Proving that simplicity and richness can coexist effortlessly. Hey. Hey. Hey. Bofilic homes are not just visually appealing, they promote health. Studies show reduced stress, improved sleep, and enhanced creativity in spaces infused with natural elements. Living among greenery and light fosters emotional stability. In essence, biofilic design is not only about aesthetics. It's about designing a home that heals. Modern living thrives on technology, but it doesn't have to clash with bofilic design. Smart lighting that mimics sunlight, climate systems that encourage natural ventilation, and automated water features enhance the experience. When tech works in harmony with natural elements, homes achieve both innovation and timeless organic warmth. Even in compact homes or apartments. Bofilic principles flourish. Vertical gardens, potted plants, light reflecting mirrors, and multifunctional wooden furniture bring nature to tight quarters. It's proof that luxury living isn't about size, but about intentional design. Even a single plant-filled balcony can transform daily life into a natural retreat. Bofilic design naturally supports sustainability. Using reclaimed wood, recycled stone, and energyefficient windows reduces the home's carbon footprint. Growing herbs indoors or choosing local materials deepens the eco-conscious lifestyle. Sustainability ensures that while we bring nature into our homes, we also protect and preserve it for future generations. Bedrooms are sanctuaries and biofilic design enhances their restorative quality. Soft natural fabrics, gentle lighting, and potted greenery create a calming atmosphere. Placing the bed near windows maximizes morning light, while earthy tones invite relaxation. The result is a personal haven that rejuvenates both body and mind The living room often reflects the heart of a home. Incorporating natural materials like stone coffee tables, rotan chairs, and large leafy plants makes the space welcoming. An indooroutdoor connection through terraces or gardens elevates the atmosphere, turning the living room into a lively, grounded, and luxurious social hub. Kitchens thrive when designed with nature in mind. Open shelving with ceramic jars, herb gardens by the window, and wooden countertops transform kitchens into nurturing spaces. Beyond aesthetics, natural light and airflow reduce energy use, making the kitchen both eco-friendly and emotionally fulfilling. The true green heart of a home. Bathrooms become wellness sanctuaries when infused with bofilic elements. Stone basins, wooden accents, and greenery turn them into spa-like retreats. Skylights and open showers enhance natural light and air circulation, creating a refreshing, luxurious atmosphere. A bathroom designed with nature not only cleanses the body, but rejuvenates the spirit. Rooftop gardens and landscape terraces provide fresh air and open relaxation. Designing with native plants ensures lowmaintenance while supporting biodiversity. These outdoor spaces become sanctuaries of joy, seamlessly blending modern living with timeless natural beauty. The presence of plants, water, and sunlight creates joy and grounding. These emotional benefits ripple into every aspect of life, improving productivity, relationships, and overall well-being. Living with nature truly enriches the human experience in profound ways. As more people value sustainability and wellness, Bofilia offers a timeless solution. Homes that embody this philosophy will remain relevant, valuable, and desirable for generations. It's a legacy of beauty, health, and harmony with nature. Less by integrating light, air, greenery and natural materials, we create homes that are alive, healing, and inspiring. In a modern world that often distances us from the earth, biophilic homes bring us back to what truly matters, harmony with nature.

- Home Interior Design for Better Mental Wellbeing: Kind: captions Language: en In today's video, I'd like to discuss how your home design can influence your mental well-being and then dive deep into various interior design techniques that support each of them. But first, I'd like to define what mental well-being is and why should our home design have any influence on it. Mental well-being is about more than just feeling happy is how you cope with daily life and what you believe you can achieve. You can have good mental well-being even if you have a mental health issue, and vice versa. When your wellbeing is high, everyday tasks become easier. So creating an environment that contributes to how well you deal with your daily difficulties is very important. Now why should your home have any impact on your mental well-being? Because your home has an impact on how you feel, particularly on how stressed or relaxed you feel. Your home design also impacts your habits, Meaning that you are much more likely to exercise the activities for which you have created space in your home And the items you surround yourself with become triggers and reminders to perform certain activities. Mindfulness Nature. One way to take care of our mental well-being is the practice of mindfulness. Paying more attention to the present moment, to your thoughts, feelings and your body can help you be more mindful. But this attention can also span to our environment, particularly to the natural environment which has been found in a series of studies since the 1980s to have a restorative role for our mind. The attention restoration theory developed by Rachel and Stephen Kaplan, basically states that looking at natural elements helps your mind relax and wonder. This break helps your mind recharge and focus better on other tasks. So bringing natural elements inside can help reduce stress and improve your mood. Interacting with any plants has a positive impact on our wellbeing. But some plants have additional benefits. Some of them are very easy to care for and remove air toxins, like the Snake plant. Others are very fragrant, like the Lavender and Rosemary, two plants who have been shown to be natural anxiety busters. They are also used in teas, food and oils, contributing to our wellbeing far beyond just seeing. Another way to bring nature in is to use natural materials Adding natural textures changes the somewhat flat and monotonous. environment we have created for ourselves. They catch your attention and touching them brings your attention to the present moment. Try to interrupt the flat and smooth surfaces around you with textures that would create a bit of surprise and delight. You might bring a sheepskin throw or a yarn cushion on your couch. you might use wooden slats to accentuate the certain part of the room. Stone can provide a natural wall texture that can be very pleasant to the touch. Ceramics can be delightful home decor elements that provide a different texture. cane can also be really interesting. In the interior. Old tables from natural raw wood that keep their ripples and crevices can provide a pleasant experience to the touch. Wooly rugs with their big knots provide a pleasant experience for our feet. So all these elements contribute to reconnecting ourselves, to nature inside our homes, our senses of smell and touch bring us back to the present moment, restoring our attention and sense of well-being. Ensure proper lighting. Light has also been extensively studied and proven to have direct impact on how we feel. It has been shown to influence our serotonin levels, which is a hormone that helps you feel happier and more relaxed. Therefore, getting sufficient amount of light when we are indoors is very important to our wellbeing. So if you live in a home that maybe doesn't get much light to begin with, you might want to maximize the amount that spreads around the room by using clear and light colored curtains, as well as pastel furnishings to keep the atmosphere as bright as possible. The color of the light bulb matters too. Warm colors make spaces feel cozy. They turn down the mood, helping us wind down and get ready for bed. While cool colors are better for focus and work during the day. Learning with your hands. This idea comes from a collection of studies showing that learning a new skill, being engaged in a creative project and working with your hands improves mental wellbeing. So engaging in regular creative hobbies, particularly one that involves using your hands like painting, knitting or carpentry helps improve cognitive function. Working with your hands on tasks that are not cognitively demanding, helps the mind rest and restore. The other idea that you need to keep in mind is that the more things you need to set up before starting a new habit, the more unlikely it is for you to do it. So if you wanted to make sure that you stayed consistent with some kind of creative habit at home, then you would have to create a physical space for it. And have everything ready for you to start. If you have to pull things out of boxes and prep items every time you had to start, all these activities would become part of your resistance towards exercising that habit. So where you want to start is to figure out the spot in your home where you can set up a table no matter how small. That would allow you to sit down and have an hour with your creative habit And I would encourage you to make it exclusively for that habit so that all your tools and devices would be laid out. now, if you don't have much space, you might make use of a corner by placing two tables next to each other, one for your home office activities and one for your creative hobby. If this is a very messy creative habit, you might want to separate it from the rest of your home with a wall. That way, even if you keep your workspace a bit messy, you will have to look at it every day. You might repurpose a closet into a small office or perhaps the garage. That way you can have a room where you can express your creativity with no restrictions. So designating spaces in your home for your creative pursuits will ensure that you have the best chance to do it regularly maintaining your mental wellbeing. Connect with others. Cultivating your relationships is another factor that is highly influential for your well-being. Relationships with other people help you build a sense of belonging and self-worth and provide emotional support and allow you to support others. When it comes to your home designing spaces that make it easy for your family to connect and exchange ideas with one another is important. But what is also important is for you to design spaces that make you feel comfortable to invite friends and family over and they don't have to be expensive or elaborate. Here are two ways you can do that. You can either bring people together around the table or you can bring them together around a seating arrangement. So having a large table is always very useful for family meals, but it can also be a gathering place for doing homework in the evenings, playing board games or working on a crafting project. It's always nice to be able to gather around the table and do something together. When picking a table that invites people to sit together, you might look for a round one, an oval one, or perhaps one with big round corners. Rectangular table is generally make people are more conscious about hierarchy and personal space at the table, but round tables or tables with round corners blurr boundaries, it's never quite clear where one side of the table ends and the other one begins. So more people can be huddles together casually sharing a space. If you combine this table with a bench, then it doesn't occupy too much space in the room. You can also use an expandable table and foldable chairs for when you do have more people over. But like with the examples before, a table with its own designated space will be occupied more frequently because it doesn't require so much prior preparation. Similarly, if you opt for a more flexible, lighter, modular sofa for the living room, you can find more configurations in which to be with your family and your friends. You can push the sofa elements around. You can group people or kids in different ways, which makes the coming together easier. Being active. Staying active is not just good for your muscles and your heart. It also improves your wellbeing by raising your self-esteem, setting, enriching goals, and by sparking changes in your brain that make you feel calm and upbeat. Now, ideally, you would want to exercise outside because it also helps you get fresh air and sunlight, which are also highly important for your wellbeing. But should you wish to exercise in your home, there are a couple of things to consider. The first thing you want to do in staying active at home is to limit the sitting options. Not in a way that is uncomfortable but in a way that you don't feel invited to sit for more than you have to. So if you have a couch, make it smaller, make it modular, if it's not massive, it will not become the primary activity in your living room. Use standing tables for a quick morning breakfast to limit your sitting time. If you have a home office, make the table height adjustable to be able to stand on occasion. Just like the previous habits, you want to make sure you can designate an area or a corner of your room to stay active and that you make it easy to start the routine with no friction. You want to have your exercise equipment easily accessible, but not in a way that makes your home look like a gym. Unless that is a gym room, in which case it's okay. If you do yoga at home, for example, you can add your mat to a beautiful wicker basket that will blend well with your couch environment. You can even deliberately buy your training equipment in colors that match your home decor. In this case, for example, the exercise ball and the yoga mat in Cork material blend perfectly with the rest of the home decor on the shelves. So these are a couple of ideas that will help you stay active at home and will help you maintain your mental wellbeing with no extra effort or willpower. If you like these ideas, you should definitely check out my video Home Design for better habits where I dive deeper into this topic. See you in the next video.

- How Luxury Brands Make You Pay $1500 for a $40 Product—and Why You Thank Them: Kind: captions Language: en This bag costs $1,500. The materials, labor, and packaging cost $38. That's a 3,800% markup. And every year, millions of people buy it. Not despite knowing this, but because of it. Today, we're going to explain exactly why. You're not paying $1,500 for leather and thread. You're paying $1,500 for a feeling. The feeling of being someone. Luxury brands don't sell products. They sell identity. And identity, it turns out, is the most expensive thing a human being will ever buy. By the end of this video, you will understand the complete psychological architecture of luxury. Why waiting lists increase desire. Why price increases drive demand. Why the bag matters more to someone who can't afford it than to someone who can. This is the most sophisticated manipulation in consumer history. In standard economics, higher prices reduce demand. Luxury operates on a completely different law called Veblen goods. For these products, raising the price increases desire. When Hermes raises Birkin prices, the waitlist gets longer. The price is the product. Unaffordability is the feature. The markup on luxury goods is not about material quality. It's rarely even two times better than premium non-luxury alternatives. The multiplier is entirely psychological. A Rolex keeps time no better than a $200 Seiko. The $8,800 difference buys nothing functional. It buys everything social. The Hermes Birkin has outperformed the S&P 500 and gold as an investment over the past 30 years. Average annual appreciation, 14.2%. This is not an accident. It's the result of engineered scarcity, cultural cachet, and a waitlist so exclusive that money alone cannot get you one. Economist Thorstein Veblen first described this in 1899. Conspicuous consumption. Buying to signal to others. A luxury item is a communication device. It broadcasts your tribe, your income, your taste, your access. The product is the signal. The signal is the product. You're not buying a bag. You're buying a sentence in a social language. Multiple studies confirm, carrying a visible luxury brand changes how strangers evaluate you. You're rated as more competent, more successful, more trustworthy, even when everything else is identical. The brand creates a halo effect that alters other people's perception of your entire personality. You're not just buying a bag. You're buying a first impression. You cannot walk into Hermes and buy a Birkin. You have to prove yourself worthy first by purchasing other items, building a relationship, demonstrating taste. The rejection is not a barrier to the The rejection is the product. Being denied access makes the desire more intense than any advertisement ever could. The dirty secret of luxury. The majority of revenue comes from people who can't actually afford it. Chanel lipstick at $50, Dior perfume at $120, a Louis Vuitton keychain at $300. These are the entry drugs. They give the middle class access to the logo without access to the real thing. Their aspiration funds the exclusivity of the top. Here's the inversion. When you wear a visible luxury logo, you become an unpaid billboard for the brand. You paid $1,200 to advertise their product to everyone you encounter. The most visible logos, LV Monogram, Gucci GG, Burberry Check, are not the most luxurious items. They're the brand's most effective advertising medium, worn by people who paid for the privilege. In 2018, Burberry was caught burning $37 million worth of unsold clothing and accessories, deliberately. Louis Vuitton has never had a sale and never will. Hermès destroys unsold bags rather than discount them. This isn't waste, it's strategy. Scarcity is more valuable than revenue. The moment luxury goes on sale, it stops being luxury. A new Rolex costs $9,000 at retail. On the secondary market, the same watch sells for $12,000 to $14,000 without ever being worn. Rolex produces fewer watches than the market demands, deliberately. The result? Owning a Rolex communicates not just wealth, but access. You didn't just spend money, you got in. Supreme sells a plain red brick with their logo for $1,000. It sold out instantly. This proves the thesis completely. The object is irrelevant. The signal is everything. Supreme took the luxury playbook, artificial scarcity, community access, tribal identity, and applied it to streetwear. The price is arbitrary. The belonging is priceless. Chanel No. 5 costs roughly $30 to produce. It sells for $200. Marilyn Monroe said she wore nothing to bed but a few drops of it. That sentence added more value to the product than any chemist ever could. The formula in the bottle is worth $30. The mythology attached to the bottle is worth $170. You're not buying fragrance. You're buying a story. Psychologist Russell Belk's research showed that we extend our sense of self into our possessions. The things we own become part of who we are. A luxury purchase is not a transaction. It's a self-definition act. When you buy a Chanel bag, you're not buying leather. You're editing your own identity. The brand gave you the story. You paid to live in it. Inside a luxury flagship, you're not a customer. You're a guest. White-gloved service, champagne, private rooms, your name remembered across visits. The purchase experience is engineered to make you feel like the exception, the chosen one. You're being made to feel wealthy, regardless of whether you are. And that feeling, not the product, is what you're actually paying for. Maslow's hierarchy puts belonging and esteem above basic survival. These are not shallow needs. They are among the most fundamental human drives. Luxury brands have figured out how to sell belonging in a box. When you buy a luxury item, you are buying access to a tribe, an identity, and the esteem that comes with it. The price is high because the need is real. Knowing all of this doesn't make luxury meaningless. Identity needs are real. Belonging is real. Esteem is real. The question isn't whether luxury brands manipulate you. They do. The question is, are you buying consciously, knowing exactly what you're paying for. Because there's nothing wrong with paying for identity, as long as you know that's what you're doing. I want your most honest answer, not the one that sounds smart, the real one. What's the most expensive thing you've ever bought purely for how it made you feel? A watch, a bag, a car, a suit, a piece of jewelry? And here's the real question. Was it worth it? Drop it below. I genuinely want to know, and I read every single one. Subscribe. Next week, social media and the aspiration economy. How platforms turn the desire for luxury into a monetizable addiction. How much it costs the average person per year, and the specific feed design that makes comparison spending almost impossible to resist. See you there.
